\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\quad\textsc{R-R}

Figure 16
.

Set of mixed nodes reflecting the
compilation scheme from
Definition
D.1
.

Figure
16

shows our choice for the set of permissible mixed nodes.
The nodes are depicted as domino-shaped boxes
where the left component stores
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
events
and the right component stores
Ex86
events.
We use the symbol
\bot
to denote an empty set of events
(in addition to its meaning as the
none
element
of an option type).
It is easy to see how this definition mimics
the compilation scheme from Definition
D.1
.
Indeed, Node
R-R
reflects how read instructions
are compiled to plain reads.
Node
W-WMF
reflects how a
sc
write
is compiled to a plain write followed by a memory fence.
Moreover, node
F-
\bot
reflects how fences weaker than
sf
are erased by the compilation scheme.
Nodes
W-NT
and
F-SF
reflects the compilation of inline-assembly instructions.
Finally, nodes
RMW-RMW-S
and
RMW-RMW-F
reflect the compilation of read-modify-writes.

To see an example of a mixed execution graph
constructed with these nodes,
consider the following program:

p\;\triangleq\;\left(\begin{array}[]{l@{~~~}||@{~~~}l}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\texttt{[}}x{\texttt{]}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}{\texttt{:=}}\,1{\texttt{;}}\\
r{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}y{\texttt{]}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}\end{array}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \vrule\vrule\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ &\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\texttt{[}}y{\texttt{]}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}{\texttt{:=}}\,1{\texttt{;}}\\
r{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}x{\texttt{]}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}\end{array}\end{array}\right)

This program implements the
store-buffering litmus test
(SB).
SB is one of the simplest demonstrations of
non-sequentially consistent behaviors:
it would happen if both read instructions returned
the value
0
.
The
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-lang program
p
, however,
exhibits only sequentially consistent behaviors
because the access mode of all memory instructions is
sc
.
The following mixed execution graph allows us to see
simultaneously
how
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
rules out SB in
p
and
how
Ex86
rules out SB in the compilation of
p
,
the program
\llparenthesis\hskip 2.0ptp\hskip 1.0pt\rrparenthesis
:

To show that SB is ruled out (in both source and compiled programs),
we must show that both
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
and
Ex86
graphs are inconsistent.
Indeed,
both graphs are inconsistent because of the cycle
(a,\,b,\,c,\,d)
.
In the
Ex86
graph, this cycle violates Condition
External
.
In the
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
graph, this cycle violates Condition
SC
.
Here is a summary of the technical arguments sustaining these claims:

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}c}\begin{array}[]{@{}l}\text{Cycle in}\;{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}\,\text{:}\\[2.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{@{\;}l}\bullet\;\left(a,\,b\right),\,\left(c,\,d\right)\in{[{\texttt{E}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,{[{\texttt{E}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}_{\textnormal{{base}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}\\[2.0pt]
\bullet\;\left(b,\,c\right),\,\left(d,\,a\right)\in{[{\texttt{E}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]};\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}};\,{[{\texttt{E}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}_{\textnormal{{base}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}\end{array}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{}l}\text{Cycle in}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e}\,\text{:}\\[2.0pt]
\begin{array}[]{@{\;}l}\bullet\;\left(a,\,b\right),\,\left(c,\,d\right)\in\textsf{Ex86}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\\[2.0pt]
\bullet\;\left(b,\,c\right),\,\left(d,\,a\right)\in{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

At first glance,
the outlined arguments might seem
informal because the relations so specified
apply only to events of a specific model,
not to events of the mixed graph.
However,
we show that these arguments can be made valid:
in essence, it suffices to lift the relations from
source and target models to the structure of mixed graphs.
That is, when working with mixed graphs,
we manipulate custom versions of these relations
defined as relations on mixed nodes.

The following definition formally introduces mixed execution graphs
and its custom version of the relations
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}
:

Definition D.3 (Mixed Execution Graph).

A
mixed execution graph
is a graph where every node,
called a
mixed node
,
is a pair of a set of
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
events
and a set of
Ex86
events.
Figure
16

depicts the set of mixed nodes allowed in a mixed graph.
The two basic relations are
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}
:

(1)

Reads-from (
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}
).

The reads-from relation is a surjective and functional
relation with domain and codomain specified as follows:

(2)

Modification-order (
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}
).

The modification-order has domain and codomain specified as follows:

We introduce the following sets of mixed nodes:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\hspace{2cm}}r@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}{\texttt{W}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{W-W}{{W-W}}} }\,\cup\,\textnormal{{\hyperlink{W-WMF}{{W-WMF}}} }\,\cup\,\textnormal{{\hyperlink{W-NT}{{W-NT}}} }\\
{\texttt{RMW}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{RMW-RMW-S}{{RMW-RMW-S}}} }\,\cup\,\textnormal{{\hyperlink{RMW-RMW-F}{{RMW-RMW-F}}} }\\
{\texttt{R}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{R-R}{{R-R}}} }\\
{\texttt{F}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{F-MF}{{F-MF}}} }\,\cup\,\textnormal{{\hyperlink{F-SF}{{F-SF}}} }\,\cup\,\textnormal{{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{{F-$\bot$}}} }\end{array}\hfil\hskip 56.9055pt&\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}{\texttt{NT}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{W-NT}{{W-NT}}} }\\
{\texttt{SF}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{F-SF}{{F-SF}}} }\\
{\texttt{MF}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{F-MF}{{F-MF}}} }\\
&&\end{array}\end{array}

When applicable,
we annotate sets of mixed nodes with superscripts of the form
“
\sqsupseteq\mathit{md}
” (and variations of it)
to specify the range of access modes
of the
RC11
events in the left component of mixed nodes.

Naturally,
reasoning at the level of mixed graphs and its corresponding
version of the relations
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}
leads to
facts about mixed graphs and nodes;
to extract a result about the source and target models,
we provide a theorem that allows one to transfer
results between these structures.
For example, we prove that,
if the consistency conditions of
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
hold of a mixed graph,
then it also holds of the source graph.

Before we introduce this theorem,
let us clarify the notions of

source graph
and
target graph
.
These concepts are not yet well defined,
because, given a mixed graph,
we have not explained how they can be obtained.
The missing piece of information is the notion of

source
and
target projections
:
given a mixed graph
G_{m}
,
its source projection
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}

and target projection
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright

correspond to the source and target graphs
whose superposition is
G_{m}
.

Figure 17
.
Selection of projection rules.

Definition D.4 (Projections).

Let
G_{m}
be a mixed execution graph.
The
source
and
target projections
of
G_{m}
,
noted
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}
and
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright
,
are
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
and
Ex86
execution graphs.
The nodes of
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}
and
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright
correspond
to the first and second parts of
G_{m}
nodes.
The edges of
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}
and
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright

are constructed through
projection rules
.
A projection rule formalizes the correspondence
between edges in
G_{m}

(appearing at the top of the rule)
and
the edges in
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}
and
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright

(appearing respectively at the bottom left
and at the bottom right of the rule).
Figure
17

shows a selection of the projection rules.

Definition D.5 (Mixed Execution Graph -
Ex86
-Consistency).

A mixed execution graph

(G_{m},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}},\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}})
is
Ex86
-consistent

if the conditions from Definition
B.1
hold
when the sets and relations to their corresponding mixed-graph versions
as introduced in Definition
D.3
,
and the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
relation with the following one:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}&\;\triangleq&[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{RMW}}\cup{\texttt{MF}}\cup{\texttt{SF}}]\\
&\;\cup&[{\texttt{R}}\cup{\texttt{RMW}}\cup{\texttt{MF}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}]\\
&\;\cup&[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}];\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}}^{?};\,[{\hyperlink{W-WMF}{\textsc{W-WMF}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}]\\
&\;\cup&[{\texttt{SF}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}\setminus{\texttt{R}}]\\
&\;\cup&[{\texttt{W}}^{\neq{\texttt{nt}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{W}}^{\neq{\texttt{nt}}}]\\
&\;\cup&[{\texttt{W}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}|_{\textnormal{{loc}}};\,[{\texttt{W}}]\end{array}

Definition D.6 (Mixed Execution Graph -
RC11
-Consistency).

A mixed execution graph

(G_{m},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}},\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}})
is
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistent

if the conditions from Definition
B.8
hold
when we replace the sets and relations to their corresponding mixed-graph versions
as introduced in Definition
D.3
.

Theorem D.7 (Transfer Principle).

Let
(G_{m},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}},\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}})
be a mixed execution graph.
The consistency conditions from
Definition
D.6

hold of
G_{m}
if, and only if,
the
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistency conditions
(Definition
B.8
)
hold of
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}
.
Analogously,
the consistency conditions from
Definition
D.5

hold of
G_{m}
if, and only if,
the
Ex86
-consistency conditions
(Definition
B.1
)
hold of
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright
.

Proof.

The first claim,
that
G_{m}
is
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistent
iff
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}
is
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistent,
is easy to see because every mixed node carries exactly
one
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
event.
Therefore, the projected nodes and relations
can be related by a one-to-one correspondence.
Since the consistency conditions
from Definition
D.6

are essentially the same as those from
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
(Definition
B.8
),
this observation is sufficient to establish this claim.

The second claim,
that
G_{m}
is
Ex86
-consistent
iff
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright
is
Ex86
-consistent,
is slightly more intricate to prove than the previous one
because of the nodes
F-
\bot
and
W-WMF
,
which do not have a one-to-one correspondence
with the projected
Ex86
events;
and because of the differences between
the two versions of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
from
Definitions

B.1

and

D.5
.
That the
Ex86
-consistency of
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright

implies that of
G_{m}

follows from the fact that
the projection of every
edge in
G_{m}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
is an edge in
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
.
Indeed, it is easy to see that the two
problematic types of nodes,
F-
\bot
and
W-WMF
,
are correctly handled by the definition of
G_{m}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
:
nodes of type
F-
\bot
are excluded from
G_{m}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
,
and
nodes of type
W-WMF
always have a trailing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}

edge so that a memory fence is always between the
two endpoints of the resulting projected edge.
This concludes one direction of the logical equivalence.
To prove the converse,
that the
Ex86
-consistency of
G_{m}

implies that of
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright
,
it suffices to show that every
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{+}

edge in a cycle that violates
External
is the projection of a
G_{m}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{+}
edge.
This condition can be easily checked;
the only non-trivial case is
when there is a memory-fence event between the endpoints of
a
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{+}
edge,
because this memory fence could be the projection
of either a
F-MF
node or a
W-WMF
node.
In both cases,
it is easy to see that the
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{+}

edge is the projection of an edge of type

G_{m}.([{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}];\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}}^{?};\,[{\texttt{MF}}\cup{\hyperlink{W-WMF}{\textsc{W-WMF}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}])
,
which is included in
G_{m}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{+}
.
∎

To conclude this discussion,
we introduce the notion of
graph simulation
,
an auxiliary concept for our upcoming compilation-correctness
proofs (
§

D.3
):

Definition D.8 (Graph Simulation).

A
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
graph
G
is
simulated by

a
Ex86
graph
G^{\prime}
,
noted
G\sim G^{\prime}
,
if there exists a mixed execution graph
G_{m}

such that
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}=G
and
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright=G^{\prime}
.

D.3.
Compilation Correctness

We state and prove correctness of the
compilation schemes from
Definitions
D.1

and
D.2
.
The statement of correctness is straightforward:

Theorem D.9 (Correctness of Definition
D.1
).

For every program
p
,
the set of behaviors of
\llparenthesis\hskip 2.0ptp\hskip 1.0pt\rrparenthesis

defined by
Ex86
is included in the set of behaviors of
p

defined by
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
:

\forall p.\;\llbracket\hskip 2.0pt{\llparenthesis\hskip 2.0ptp\hskip 1.0pt\rrparenthesis}\hskip 1.0pt\rrbracket_{\textsf{Ex86}}\subseteq\llbracket\hskip 2.0pt{p}\hskip 1.0pt\rrbracket_{\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}}

Theorem D.10 (Correctness of Definition
D.2
).

Statement analogous to Theorem
D.9

D.3.1.
Proof Sketch.

The overall structure of our proofs
is depicted by the following diagram:

It illustrates the first step
of a two-steps strategy to prove that
\llparenthesis\hskip 2.0pt\_\hskip 1.0pt\rrparenthesis
is correct.

This first step consists of showing that,
for every program
p
,
for every execution graph
G^{\prime}

associated with
\llparenthesis\hskip 2.0ptp\hskip 1.0pt\rrparenthesis
,
there exists a graph
G
associated with
p
,
such that
G\sim G^{\prime}
.

The second step is then to show that,
if the consistency conditions
from the target model hold of a mixed execution graph
G_{m}
,
then the consistency conditions
from the source model also hold of
G_{m}
.
Finally,
by invoking Theorem
D.7
,
it follows that,
if
G^{\prime}
is consistent
(with respect to the target model),
then
G
is consistent
(with respect to the source model).
In particular,
this implies that
every final state of
\llparenthesis\hskip 2.0ptp\hskip 1.0pt\rrparenthesis

is a final state of
p
,
which statement corresponds precisely
to the formulation of compilation correctness.

The first step is accomplished by induction over
the construction of the graph
G^{\prime}
.
Intuitively,
because the compiled program
\llparenthesis\hskip 2.0ptp\hskip 1.0pt\rrparenthesis

preserves much of the structure of
p
,
it is possible to replay the pool-reduction steps
(Figures
8

and
9
)
from
\llparenthesis\hskip 2.0ptp\hskip 1.0pt\rrparenthesis
and yield a graph
G

that satisfies the desired properties.

The second step is the crux of our proofs and
it is where we concentrate our attention.
Next, we discuss how to accomplish this
step in the case of
Ex86
,
first considering the standard compilation scheme
(Definition
D.1
)
and then the alternative one
(Definition
D.2
).

D.3.2.
Compilation to
Ex86
.

Lemma D.11 (
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-Weaker-Than-
Ex86
).

Let
G_{m}
be a mixed execution graph.
If
G_{m}
is
Ex86
-consistent,
then
G_{m}
is
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistent.

Proof.

Suppose that
G_{m}
is
Ex86
-consistent
but not
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistent.
Then at least one of
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
consistency conditions must
not hold of
G_{m}
.
We show that the violation of any of them
leads to a contradiction:

(1)

Coherence-I
.

The violation of
Coherence-I
implies that (at least)
one of the following assertions holds:

(a)

Assertion:
\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}})

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}})&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\\
&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}((\underbrace{[\mathit{codom}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}}_{\subseteq\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}})\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\\
&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}

(b)

Assertion:
\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\\
&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}

(c)

Assertion:
\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}))

\begin{array}[]{@{~~~~~~~}l@{}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \lx@intercol\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{i}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{i}))&\implies&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})}^{+};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{i}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{i}))\\
&\implies&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})}^{+};\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}})\\
&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\\
&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \lx@intercol\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\\[2.84526pt]
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \lx@intercol\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}l@{}}&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e}))\\
&\implies\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0}\sf{sw}})}^{+};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e}))\implies\\
&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{l}\overbrace{[\mathit{codom}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}})];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}};\,[{\texttt{E}}^{\sqsupseteq{\texttt{rel}}}];\,{([{\texttt{F}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}})}^{?};\,[{\texttt{W}}^{\sqsupseteq{\texttt{rlx}}}]}^{\begin{array}[]{l}\subseteq\;\left(\begin{array}[]{l}[{\texttt{W}}^{\neq{\texttt{nt}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{W}}^{\neq{\texttt{nt}}}]\;\cup\\
[{\texttt{W}}^{{\texttt{nt}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}|_{\textnormal{{loc}}};\,[{\texttt{W}}]\;\cup\\
[{\texttt{W}}^{{\texttt{nt}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{RMW}}^{{\texttt{tso}}}\cup{\texttt{F}}^{\sqsupseteq{\texttt{sf}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}\end{array}\right)\;\subseteq\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\end{array}};\\
{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{+};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}\end{array}\right)\\
&\implies\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}};\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{+};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e}))\\
&\implies\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}\end{array}

(d)

Assertion:
\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}});\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})

\begin{array}[]{@{~~~~}l@{}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \lx@intercol\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{i}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{i});\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})&\implies&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\\
&\implies&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\end{array}\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \lx@intercol\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \lx@intercol\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}l@{}}&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e});\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\\
&\implies\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}\cup{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})}^{*};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e});\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\\
&\implies\neg\mathit{irreflexive}([\mathit{codom}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}})];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}}^{?})}^{*};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e});\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\\
&\implies\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}};\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e});\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\\
&\implies\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}\end{array}

(2)

Coherence-II
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}_{\textnormal{{asm}}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.9,0,0}\sf{eco}})&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}_{\textnormal{{asm}}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}})\\
&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}(({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}_{\textnormal{{asm}}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{i}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{i})\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\\
&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}

(3)

Coherence-III
. (Immediate by
Internal
.)

(4)

Atomicity
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}};\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}})&\implies&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{i};\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{i})\;\lor\;\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e})\\
&\implies&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}})\;\lor\;\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}

(5)

SC
.

We prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}\subseteq{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}}}^{+}
,
therefore
\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}})\implies\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}})
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}&\;\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}_{\textnormal{{fence}}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}_{\textnormal{{base}}}\\[5.69054pt]
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}_{\textnormal{{fence}}}&\;\triangleq&[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,{\color[rgb]{0.9,0,0}\sf{eco}};\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}});\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]\\
&\;=&[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]\,\cup\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,{\color[rgb]{0.9,0,0}\sf{eco}};\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]\end{array}\\
\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\\[5.69054pt]
\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]&\subseteq&\underbrace{[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,}_{\subseteq\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}})}^{*}\subseteq{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})}^{+}\subseteq{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}}}^{+}\end{array}\\
\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\\[5.69054pt]
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,{\color[rgb]{0.9,0,0}\sf{eco}};\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]\\
\quad\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\subseteq\;\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\overbrace{[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}}^{\subseteq\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}};\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*};\,\overbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.9,0,0}\sf{eco}}}^{\subseteq\;{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}}}^{+}};\\
\underbrace{[\mathit{codom}({\color[rgb]{0.9,0,0}\sf{eco}})];\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}}^{?};\,{([{\texttt{E}}^{\sqsupseteq{\texttt{rel}}}];\,{([{\texttt{F}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}})}^{?};\,[{\texttt{W}}^{\sqsupseteq{\texttt{rlx}}}];\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*})}^{?};\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]}_{\begin{array}[]{l}\subseteq\;\left(\begin{array}[]{l}[{\texttt{R}}\cup{\texttt{RMW}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*}\;\cup\\
[{\texttt{W}}^{\neq{\texttt{nt}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{W}}^{\neq{\texttt{nt}}}\cup{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*}\;\cup\\
[{\texttt{W}}^{{\texttt{nt}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}|_{\textnormal{{loc}}};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\texttt{R}}];\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*}\;\cup\\
[{\texttt{W}}^{{\texttt{nt}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{RMW}}^{{\texttt{tso}}}\cup{\texttt{F}}^{\sqsupseteq{\texttt{sf}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\texttt{R}}];\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*}\end{array}\right)^{?}\end{array}}\end{array}\\
\subseteq\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}};\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*};\,{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}}}^{+};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?};\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*}\;\subseteq\;{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}}}^{+}\end{array}\par\end{array}\\
\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\\[5.69054pt]
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\sf{psc}}_{\textnormal{{base}}}\;\triangleq\;\begin{array}[]{@{\;}l@{}}([{\texttt{E}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]\,\cup\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}}^{?});\\
\quad({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}|_{\neq\textnormal{{loc}}};\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}|_{\neq\textnormal{{loc}}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}|_{\textnormal{{loc}}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}});\\
({{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}}^{?};\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]\,\cup\,[{\texttt{E}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}])\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\;}ll@{}}\subseteq&[{\texttt{E}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})}^{+}&(\subseteq[{\texttt{E}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*}\subseteq{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}}}^{+})\\
\cup&[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,{\color[rgb]{0.9,0,0}\sf{eco}};\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]&(\subseteq{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}}}^{+})\\
\cup&[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}})&(\subseteq[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{*};\,{\color[rgb]{0.9,0,0}\sf{eco}}\subseteq{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}}}^{+})\\
\cup&({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}});\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]&(\text{See proof of}\;[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,{\color[rgb]{0.9,0,0}\sf{eco}};\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}]\subseteq{{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}}}^{+})\\
\cup&({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}})&(\subseteq({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{i}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{i})\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.2,0.2,1}\sf{ob}})\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

(6)

No-Thin-Air
.

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}(\underbrace{([\mathit{codom}({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}})];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}})}_{\subseteq\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e})\\
&\implies&\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}

∎

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\quad\textsc{F-SF-alt}

Figure 18
.

Selected set of mixed nodes reflecting the
alternative compilation scheme from
Definition
D.2
.

To apply our methodology of mixed execution graphs
to show the correctness of the alternative compilation scheme
(Definition
D.2
),
we need to complete the three following preliminary steps:

(1)

Define the set of mixed nodes that reflect
the alternative compilation scheme.

(2)

State
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistency and
Ex86
-consistency
of mixed execution graphs containing this new set of nodes.

(3)

Prove the transfer principle for these new consistency
definitions.

Figure
18

depicts a selection of the updated mixed nodes
reflecting the alternative compilation scheme.
The complete set of nodes is the same as
Figure
16

with the following exceptions:
(1) nodes of type
W-WMF
are replaced with
W-SFWMF
,
(2) nodes of type
F-
\bot
with access mode
{\texttt{rel}}/{\texttt{acqrel}}

are replaced with
F-SF-alt
, and
(3) nodes of type
W-W
with access modes
rlx
and
rel

are respectively replaced with
W-NT-alt
and
W-SFW
.

To make the distinction between
mixed graphs composed of nodes
as defined in Figure
16

and
mixed graphs composed of nodes
as defined in Figure
18

clear,
we call the later
alternative mixed execution graphs
.
To state
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistency for alternative mixed graphs,
it suffices to update the notation introduced in
Definition
D.5
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\hspace{2cm}}r@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}{\texttt{W}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{W-W}{{W-W}}} }\,\cup\,\hbox{\pagecolor{green!40!black!08}$\textnormal{{\hyperlink{W-SFWMF}{{W-SFWMF}}} }$\/}\,\cup\,\textnormal{{\hyperlink{W-NT-alt}{{W-NT-alt}}} }\\
{\texttt{RMW}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{RMW-RMW-S}{{RMW-RMW-S}}} }\,\cup\,\textnormal{{\hyperlink{RMW-RMW-F}{{RMW-RMW-F}}} }\\
{\texttt{R}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{R-R}{{R-R}}} }\\
{\texttt{F}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{F-MF}{{F-MF}}} }\,\cup\,\textnormal{{\hyperlink{F-SF-alt}{{F-SF-alt}}} }\,\cup\,\textnormal{{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{{F-$\bot$}}} }\end{array}\hfil\hskip 56.9055pt&\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}{\texttt{NT}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{W-NT-alt}{{W-NT-alt}}} }\\
{\texttt{SF}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{F-SF-alt}{{F-SF-alt}}} }\\
{\texttt{MF}}&\;\triangleq&\textnormal{{\hyperlink{F-MF}{{F-MF}}} }\\
&&\end{array}\end{array}

The statement of
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistency thus corresponds
to Definition
B.8

when we replace the sets and relations to their corresponding
mixed-graph versions just introduced.
The statement of
Ex86
-consistency however
needs more attention,
so we state it in a separate definition:

Definition D.12 (Alternative Mixed Execution Graph -
Ex86
-Consistency).

An alternative mixed execution graph

(G_{m},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}},\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}})
is
Ex86
-consistent

if the conditions from Definition
B.1
hold
when we replace
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}
, and the sets of nodes with the ones
just introduced and the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
relation with the following one:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;\;}l@{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}&\;\triangleq&[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{RMW}}\cup{\texttt{MF}}\cup{\texttt{SF}}\cup{\hyperlink{W-SFWMF}{\textsc{W-SFWMF}}}]\\
&\;\cup&[{\texttt{R}}\cup{\texttt{RMW}}\cup{\texttt{MF}}\cup{\hyperlink{W-SFWMF}{\textsc{W-SFWMF}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}]\\
&\;\cup&[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\hyperlink{W-SFW}{\textsc{W-SFW}}}];\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}}^{?};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}]\\
&\;\cup&[{\texttt{SF}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}\setminus{\texttt{R}}]\\
&\;\cup&[{\texttt{W}}^{\neq{\texttt{nt}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{W}}^{\neq{\texttt{nt}}}]\\
&\;\cup&[{\texttt{W}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}|_{\textnormal{{loc}}};\,[{\texttt{W}}]\end{array}

Finally, we state and prove the corresponding transfer principle
for alternative mixed graphs:

Theorem D.13 (Transfer Principle).

Let
(G_{m},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}},\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}})
be an alternative mixed execution graph.
The
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistency conditions
hold of
G_{m}
iff
they hold of
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}
.
Analogously,
the
Ex86
-consistency conditions
hold of
G_{m}
iff
they hold of
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
D.7
,
that
G_{m}
is
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistent iff
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}
is

\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistent,
is straightforward,
because there is a one-to-one correspondence
between
G_{m}
and
\downharpoonleft\hskip-3.0pt{G_{m}}
and
because
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistency conditions are
equivalently defined for both graphs.

We now prove
that
G_{m}
is
Ex86
-consistent iff
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright
is

Ex86
-consistent.
By studying the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}

from Definition
D.12
,
it is easy to see that every
G_{m}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
edge
is projected to a
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{+}
edge.
To give an example,
edges of type

G_{m}.([{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\hyperlink{W-SFW}{\textsc{W-SFW}}}];\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}}^{?};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\hyperlink{F-$\bot$}{\textsc{F-$\bot$}}}\setminus{\texttt{R}}])

are projected to edges of type

({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).(\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{SF}}]}_{\subseteq\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}};\,\underbrace{[{\texttt{SF}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{W}}];\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}}^{?};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\texttt{R}}]}_{\subseteq\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}),

who belong to
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{+}
.
Therefore, if the
Ex86
-consistency
conditions hold of
{G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright

they must hold of
G_{m}
.

To show the converse,
it suffices to check that every edge of type

R\;\triangleq\;({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).([{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\texttt{MF}}\setminus{\texttt{SF}}];\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{+};\,[{\texttt{E}}\setminus{\texttt{MF}}\setminus{\texttt{SF}}])

is the projection of an edge of type
G_{m}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{+}
.
(It is sound to restrict our attention to edges
that do not start or end in a fence,
because only this type of edge can be used to form
cycles that violate
External
.)
Let
\left(a,\,b\right)
be an edge in
R
.
The proof that
\left(a,\,b\right)
is the projection
of an edge of type
G_{m}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{+}

goes by disjunction of cases on whether there
is a fence or a read-modify-write event
between
a
and
b
.

In the negative case,
the
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
edges between
a
and
b

are of type either

({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).([\textsf{Ex86}.{\texttt{W}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[\textsf{Ex86}.{\texttt{W}}])\quad\text{or}\quad({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).([\textsf{Ex86}.({\texttt{W}}\cup{\texttt{NT}})];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}|_{\textnormal{{loc}}};\,[\textsf{Ex86}.({\texttt{W}}\cup{\texttt{NT}})]).

It is easy to see that, in this case, events in
\mathit{dom}(({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).([{\texttt{W}}\cup{\texttt{NT}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}};\,[b]))

come from the projection of mixed nodes of type either
W-W
,or
W-NT-alt
;
and that
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
edges between these nodes
correspond to either

G_{m}.([{\texttt{W}}^{\neq{\texttt{nt}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{W}}^{\neq{\texttt{nt}}}])\quad\text{or}\quad G_{m}.([{\texttt{W}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}|_{\textnormal{{loc}}};\,[{\texttt{W}}]),

both of which are included in
G_{m}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
.

In the affirmative case,
there must be at least two
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}

edges between
a
and
b
;
then the proof follows by induction on the number
n

of the remaining
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
edges.
In the inductive case,
we can assume that there is only one fence or read-modify-write event
between
a
and
b
and that this event is the target
of the immediate
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
coming out from
a
,
because, otherwise, the edge
\left(a,\,b\right)
would fit
into a smaller number of
({G_{m}}\hskip-3.0pt\downharpoonright).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
edges
and the inductive hypothesis would be applicable.
∎

Lemma D.14 (
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-Weaker-Than-
Ex86
-Alt).

Let
G_{m}
be an alternative mixed execution graph
(that is, a mixed graph formed of nodes as specified in Figure
18
).
If
G_{m}
is
Ex86
-consistent,
then
G_{m}
is
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistent.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma
D.11
.
The main difference is how we show
that the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}
prefix of a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}
edge
is included in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}
.
To give an illustration,
we include here the proof that,
in the alternative mixed graph
G_{m}
,
the violation of the condition
\mathit{irreflexive}{({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e}))}

(ensured by
Coherence-I
)
implies the violation of
External
.
The idea is to exploit the fact that,
thanks to the alternative compilation scheme
(which is encoded in the structure of
G_{m}
),
a
{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0}\sf{sw}}
edge always starts with a store fence
or a stronger barrier:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}l@{}}&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e}))\\
&\implies\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3,0}\sf{sw}})}^{+};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e}))\implies\\
&\neg\mathit{irreflexive}\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{l}\overbrace{[\mathit{codom}({\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}})];\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}}^{?};\,[{\texttt{E}}^{\sqsupseteq{\texttt{rel}}}];\,{([{\texttt{F}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}})}^{?};\,[{\texttt{W}}^{\sqsupseteq{\texttt{rlx}}}]}^{\begin{array}[]{l}\subseteq\;\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}[{\texttt{W}}^{{\texttt{nt}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}|_{\textnormal{{loc}}};\,[{\texttt{W}}]\;\cup\\
[{\texttt{W}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,\left[\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\texttt{MF}}\cup{\texttt{SF}}\cup{\texttt{RMW}}\,\cup\\
{\hyperlink{W-SFW}{\textsc{W-SFW}}}\,\cup\\
{\hyperlink{W-SFWMF}{\textsc{W-SFWMF}}}\end{array}\right];\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}}^{?};\,[{\texttt{W}}]\end{array}\right)^{?}\;\subseteq\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?}\end{array}};\\
{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{+};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}\end{array}\right)\\
&\implies\neg\mathit{irreflexive}({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?};\,{({{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e};\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}}^{?})}^{+};\,({{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e}))\\
&\implies\mathit{cyclic}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{e}\,\cup\,{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{e})\end{array}

∎

D.4.
Compiler Optimizations

D.4.1.
Register Promotion.

The following counterexample shows that
applying this optimization to inline-assembly
read-modify-writes is unsound:

\begin{array}[]{@{}ccc@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~~}||@{~~}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\texttt{{asm}}}\,{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}[x]{\texttt{:=}}_{{\texttt{nt}}}\,1{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\\
{\texttt{{asm}}}\,{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}a\,{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{rmw}}\,({\texttt{[}}z{\texttt{]}},\,0,\,1){\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\color[rgb]{0.8008,0,0}{\,\textit{//}\;\underline{0}}\\
{\texttt{[}}y{\texttt{]}}^{{\texttt{rel}}}{\texttt{:=}}\,1\end{array}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \vrule\vrule\penalty\ \penalty\ &\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}b{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}y{\texttt{]}}^{{\texttt{acq}}}\color[rgb]{0.8008,0,0}{\,\textit{//}\;\underline{1}}\\
c{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}x{\texttt{]}}^{{\texttt{rlx}}}\color[rgb]{0.8008,0,0}{\,\textit{//}\;\underline{1}}\\
\end{array}\end{array}&\rightsquigarrow&\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~~}||@{~~}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\texttt{{asm}}}\,{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}[x]{\texttt{:=}}_{{\texttt{nt}}}\,1{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\\
d{\texttt{:=}}\,0\\
a{\texttt{:=}}\,d\\
d{\texttt{:=}}\,1\\
{\texttt{[}}y{\texttt{]}}^{{\texttt{rel}}}{\texttt{:=}}\,1\end{array}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \vrule\vrule\penalty\ \penalty\ &\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}b{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}y{\texttt{]}}^{{\texttt{acq}}}\color[rgb]{0.3047,0.6055,0.0234}{\,\textit{//}\;1}\\
c{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}x{\texttt{]}}^{{\texttt{rlx}}}\color[rgb]{0.3047,0.6055,0.0234}{\,\textit{//}\;1}\\
\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Theorem D.15 (Register Promotion).

The transformation that promotes accesses to a register
a location used by only one thread and not via
inline-assembly read-modify-writes
is sound:
for every
p
and
p^{\prime}
,
if
p\rightsquigarrow p^{\prime}
,
then
\llbracket\hskip 2.0pt{p^{\prime}}\hskip 1.0pt\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\hskip 2.0pt{p}\hskip 1.0pt\rrbracket
.

Proof.

Let
z
be the location that is promoted to a register,
let
j
be the identifier of the thread
to which this location belongs,
and let
G^{\prime}
be a execution graph associated with
p^{\prime}
.
Let
G
be a graph associated with
p
,
obtained by extending
G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}
with
the missing
z
accesses
and by extending
G^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}

in such a way that
G.{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{z},{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{z},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{z}\subseteq G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}

(this is possible because
the register instructions in
p^{\prime}
,
to which the accesses to
z
were promoted,
are executed in order).
We show that, if
G^{\prime}
is
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistent,
then so is
G
,
and that, if
G^{\prime}
is racy, then so is
G
.

The proof relies on the fact that
[G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}];\,G.({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}}^{+});\,[G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}]

is included in
G^{\prime}.({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}}^{+})
.
Indeed,
let
\left(a,\,b\right)
be a pair in
G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}
,
such that
\left(a,\,b\right)\in G.({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}}^{+})
.
We show that
\left(a,\,b\right)\in G^{\prime}.({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}}^{+})
.
If
a\notin{\texttt{W}}^{{\texttt{nt}}}
, then this is clearly the case.
Now, suppose that
a\in{\texttt{W}}^{{\texttt{nt}}}
.
Consequently,
for
\left(a,\,b\right)
to be included in
G.({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}}^{+})
,
there must be an event
c\in{\texttt{RMW}}^{{\texttt{tso}}}\,\cup\,{\texttt{F}}^{\sqsupseteq{\texttt{sf}}}
,
such that
\left(a,\,c\right),\left(c,\,b\right)\in G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}
.
Such an event is not an access to
z
,
because
z
is not accessed through inline-assembly
read-modify-writes
and because a fence is not an access to
z
.
This event is thus included in
G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}
.
It is then easy to see that
\left(a,\,b\right)\in G^{\prime}.({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}}^{+})
.

From this fact, it follows that
[G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}];\,G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,[G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}]

is included in
G^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}
.

We now prove that,
if
G^{\prime}
is racy, then so is
G
.
If
G^{\prime}
is racy,
then there are
a,b\in G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}
,
such that
\left(a,\,b\right)\in{\texttt{race}}(G^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}})
.
To show that
\left(a,\,b\right)\in{\texttt{race}}(G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}})
,
it suffices to show that
\left(a,\,b\right)\notin G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}\,\cup\,G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}^{-1}
.
Suppose, by contradiction, and without loss of generality,
that
\left(a,\,b\right)\in G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}
.
Then, from the inclusion
[G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}];\,G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}};\,[G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}]\subseteq G^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}
,
it follows that
\left(a,\,b\right)\in G^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}
,
a contradiction to
\left(a,\,b\right)\in{\texttt{race}}(G^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}})
.

Now, suppose that
G^{\prime}
is
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistent.
We prove that so is
G
.
Suppose, by contradiction, this is not the case,
and let
C
be a cycle violating

\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-consistency in
G
.
This cycle must contain at least one access to
z
,
because, if every event in
C
is included in
G^{\prime}.{\texttt{E}}
,
then
C
is also a violation to
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
in
G^{\prime}
.
The cycle is either contained in thread
j

or it spans over more than one thread.
If the cycle spans over more than one thread,
then every access to
z
in
C

must be surrounded by two accesses to other locations
that are distinct from
z

(but not necessarily between themselves),
because
z
is not shared among threads.
The accesses to
z
in
C
can thus be avoided,
thereby yielding a consistency-violating cycle in
G^{\prime}
,
a contradiction to its consistency.
If the cycle is contained in thread
j
,
then there must be two accesses
a,b\in C
,
such that
\left(a,\,b\right)\in G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}

and
\left(b,\,a\right)\in G.({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}})
.
These accesses cannot be to
z
,
because
G.{{\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\sf{mo}}}_{z},{{\color[rgb]{0.7,0,0}\sf{rb}}}_{z},{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{z}\subseteq G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}
.
Therefore, this
consistency-violating pair of events also belongs to
G^{\prime}
,
a contradiction to its consistency.
∎

D.4.2.
Sequentialization.

The following counterexample shows that
sequentialization is unsound in
Ex86
(and therefore also unsound in
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
because of
Property P4
):

\begin{array}[]{@{}ccc@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~~}||@{~~}l@{~~}||@{~~}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\texttt{[}}x{\texttt{]}}{\texttt{:=}}\,1\end{array}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \vrule\vrule\penalty\ \penalty\ &\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}a{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}x{\texttt{]}}\color[rgb]{0.8008,0,0}{\,\textit{//}\;\underline{1}}\\
b{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}y{\texttt{]}}\color[rgb]{0.8008,0,0}{\,\textit{//}\;\underline{0}}\end{array}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \vrule\vrule\penalty\ \penalty\ &\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\texttt{[}}y{\texttt{]}}{\texttt{:=}}\,1\\
{\texttt{mfence}}\\
c{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}x{\texttt{]}}\color[rgb]{0.8008,0,0}{\,\textit{//}\;\underline{0}}\end{array}\end{array}&\rightsquigarrow&\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~~}||@{~~}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\texttt{[}}x{\texttt{]}}{\texttt{:=}}\,1\\
a{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}x{\texttt{]}}\color[rgb]{0.3047,0.6055,0.0234}{\,\textit{//}\;1}\\
b{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}y{\texttt{]}}\color[rgb]{0.3047,0.6055,0.0234}{\,\textit{//}\;0}\end{array}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \vrule\vrule\penalty\ \penalty\ &\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}{\texttt{[}}y{\texttt{]}}{\texttt{:=}}\,1\\
{\texttt{mfence}}\\
c{\texttt{:=}}\,{\texttt{[}}x{\texttt{]}}\color[rgb]{0.3047,0.6055,0.0234}{\,\textit{//}\;0}\end{array}\end{array}\\[28.45274pt]
\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\@add@centering\centering{\includegraphics[]{EXTERNAL_TIKZ_FOLDER/tikz_counterexample_sequentialization_program_app.pdf}}\end{array}&&\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\@add@centering\centering\includegraphics[]{EXTERNAL_TIKZ_FOLDER/tikz_counterexample_sequentialization_graph_app.pdf}\end{array}\end{array}

Theorem D.16 (NITIA-Sequentialization).

The transformation that merges two threads that satisfy NITIA is sound:
for every
p
and
p^{\prime}
,
if
p\rightsquigarrow p^{\prime}
,
then
\llbracket\hskip 2.0pt{p^{\prime}}\hskip 1.0pt\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\hskip 2.0pt{p}\hskip 1.0pt\rrbracket
.

Proof.

Let
G^{\prime}
be an execution graph associated with
p^{\prime}
,
and let
G
be an execution graph associated with
p

such that
G\rightsquigarrow G^{\prime}
.
We show that, (1) if
G^{\prime}
is consistent, then so is
G
;
and that, (2) if
G^{\prime}
is racy, then so is
G
.

The proof of (1) follows by contradiction:
suppose that
G
is
\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-inconsistent,
but
G^{\prime}
is not.
Then there exists a problematic edge between
the two threads being merged.
We saw that such an edge must contain at least
one inline-assembly event.
This contradicts the NITIA condition,
which prevents the existence of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}
edges
between such accesses.

To show (2),
it suffices to notice that
G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}
is included in
G^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}
.
Indeed, this inclusion holds
because the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}

does not distinguish the internal
from the external components of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}

and because
G.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}
is included in
G^{\prime}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}
.
∎

Theorem D.17 (Fence-Sequentialization).

The transformation that merges two threads
by inserting a
sc
fence between them
is sound:
for every
p
and
p^{\prime}
,
if
p\rightsquigarrow p^{\prime}
,
then
\llbracket\hskip 2.0pt{p^{\prime}}\hskip 1.0pt\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket\hskip 2.0pt{p}\hskip 1.0pt\rrbracket
.

Proof.

It suffices to notice that
an external edge
\left(a,\,b\right)\in G.{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}

becomes part of both
G^{\prime}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}}^{+}
and
G^{\prime}.{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}_{\textnormal{{asm}}}}^{+}
,
because
a
sc
fence is inserted between
a
and
b
:

\left(a,\,b\right)\in G^{\prime}.({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}};\,[{\texttt{F}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}];\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}})\subseteq G^{\prime}.({{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}}^{+}\cap{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}_{\textnormal{{asm}}}}^{+})
.
∎

D.4.3.
Deordering.

{\texttt{R}}^{\mathit{md}_{2}}_{y}

{\texttt{W}}^{\mathit{md}_{2}}_{y}

{\texttt{RMW}}^{\mathit{md}_{2}}_{y}

{\texttt{F}}^{\mathit{md}_{2}}

{\texttt{R}}^{\mathit{md}_{1}}_{x}

\mathit{md}_{1}\sqsubseteq{\texttt{rlx}}

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{md}_{1},\mathit{md}_{2}\sqsubseteq{\texttt{rlx}}\\[-5.0pt]
{\texttt{na}}\in\{\mathit{md}_{1},\mathit{md}_{2}\}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{md}_{1}={\texttt{na}}\\[-5.0pt]
\mathit{md}_{2}\sqsubseteq{\texttt{acq}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{md}_{1}\neq{\texttt{rlx}}\\[-5.0pt]
\mathit{md}_{2}={\texttt{acq}}\end{array}

{\texttt{W}}^{\mathit{md}_{1}}_{x}

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{md}_{1}\neq{\texttt{sc}}\\[-5.0pt]
\mathit{md}_{2}\neq{\texttt{sc}}\end{array}

\mathit{md}_{2}\sqsubseteq{\texttt{rlx}}

\mathit{md}_{2}\sqsubseteq{\texttt{acq}}

\mathit{md}_{2}={\texttt{acq}}

{\texttt{RMW}}^{\mathit{md}_{1}}_{x}

\mathit{md}_{1}\sqsubseteq{\texttt{rel}}

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{md}_{1}\sqsubseteq{\texttt{rel}}\\[-5.0pt]
\mathit{md}_{2}={\texttt{na}}\end{array}

—

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{md}_{1}\sqsupseteq{\texttt{acq}}\\[-5.0pt]
\mathit{md}_{2}={\texttt{acq}}\end{array}

{\texttt{F}}^{\mathit{md}_{1}}

\mathit{md}_{1}={\texttt{rel}}

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{md}_{1}={\texttt{rel}}\\[-5.0pt]
\mathit{md}_{2}\neq{\texttt{rlx}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{md}_{1}={\texttt{rel}}\\[-5.0pt]
\mathit{md}_{2}\sqsupseteq{\texttt{rel}}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{md}_{1}={\texttt{rel}}\\[-5.0pt]
\mathit{md}_{2}={\texttt{acq}}\end{array}

Figure 19
.
Deorderable pairs.
Events belong to
RC11
.
The variables
x
and
y
denote distinct locations.

Deordering transforms sequential composition
into parallel composition:

s{\texttt{;}}\,s^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{~~}||@{~~}l@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}s\end{array}\hfil\penalty\ \penalty\ \vrule\vrule\penalty\ \penalty\ &\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}s^{\prime}\end{array}\end{array}

Because, for simplicity,

\textsf{RC11}^{\textsf{Ex86}}
-lang does not support
the dynamic allocation of threads,
we can only express this optimization
as a transformation that converts a
single two-instructions thread into
two one-instruction threads.
At the level of execution graphs, however,
we are able to express a more general transformation
that removes a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}
edge between two given
events
a
and
b
but keeps every other
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}

edge from and to these events:

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{\quad\quad}c@{\quad\quad}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\includegraphics[]{EXTERNAL_TIKZ_FOLDER/tikz_deordering_before_app.pdf}\end{array}&\rightsquigarrow&\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\includegraphics[]{EXTERNAL_TIKZ_FOLDER/tikz_deordering_after_app.pdf}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure
19

shows the pairs
of
RC11
events
a
and
b
for which this
transformation is correct.
The Figure corresponds to Table 1 from
Lahav et al. (2017)
.
Because events are restricted to plain
RC11
,
and because every
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}_{\textnormal{{asm}}}
edge contains at least one
asm
event,
this transformation has no effect on
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}_{\textnormal{{asm}}}
.
Moreover, no external edge is removed by deordering.
Therefore,
the restriction of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}
to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\sf{rf}}}_{e}
is not problematic
and
cycles in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{ppo}}_{\textnormal{{asm}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.9,0,0}\sf{eco}}
cannot be undone
by deordering.
In sum, verifying the correctness of deordering is
limited to studying the effects of this transformation to
the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sf{po}}_{\textnormal{\scalebox{.70}{{RC11} }}}
component of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sf{hb}}
and to the cycles
that violate the standard
RC11
conditions;
it can thus follow the
same arguments as those conveyed by
Lahav et al. (2017)
.

D.4.4.
Merging.

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{\quad\quad\quad\quad}c@{\quad\quad\quad\quad}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}{\texttt{W}}^{\mathit{md}}{\texttt{;}}\,{\texttt{W}}^{\mathit{md}}&\rightsquigarrow&{\texttt{W}}^{\mathit{md}}\\
{\texttt{R}}^{\mathit{md}}{\texttt{;}}\,{\texttt{R}}^{\mathit{md}}&\rightsquigarrow&{\texttt{R}}^{\mathit{md}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}{\texttt{W}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}{\texttt{;}}\,{\texttt{R}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}&\rightsquigarrow&{\texttt{W}}^{{\texttt{sc}}}\\
{\texttt{W}}^{\mathit{md}}{\texttt{;}}\,{\texttt{R}}^{{\texttt{acq}}}&\rightsquigarrow&{\texttt{W}}^{\mathit{md}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}l@{}}{\texttt{RMW}}^{\mathit{md}}{\texttt{;}}\,{\texttt{RMW}}^{\mathit{md}}&\rightsquigarrow&{\texttt{RMW}}^{\mathit{md}}\\
{\texttt{F}}^{\mathit{md}}{\texttt{;}}\,{\texttt{F}}^{\mathit{md}}&\rightsquigarrow&{\texttt{F}}^{\mathit{md}}\end{array}\end{array}\\[14.22636pt]
\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}c@{\;}ll@{}}{\texttt{W}}^{\mathit{md}_{1}}{\texttt{;}}\,{\texttt{RMW}}^{\mathit{md}_{2}}&\rightsquigarrow&{\texttt{W}}^{\mathit{md}_{1}}&\mathit{md}_{1}=\mathit{max}\,(\mathit{md}\mid{\texttt{rlx}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{md}\sqsubseteq\mathit{md}_{2})\\
{\texttt{RMW}}^{\mathit{md}_{1}}{\texttt{;}}\,{\texttt{R}}^{\mathit{md}_{2}}&\rightsquigarrow&{\texttt{RMW}}^{\mathit{md}_{2}}&\mathit{md}_{2}=\mathit{max}\,(\mathit{md}\mid{\texttt{rlx}}\sqsubseteq\mathit{md}\sqsubseteq\mathit{md}_{1})\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 20
.
Mergeable pairs. Events belong to
RC11
. Events in a pair access the same location.

Merging transforms two consecutive instructions into one.
Figure
20
depicts the pairs of events
that can be merged.
These pairs impose constraints on the access modes of the events,
but, when performed after strengthening,
this transformation can be applied to any
pair of instructions whose accesses are at least the
ones so specified.
The remarks from the previous discussion
(about the correctness of deordering)
also apply here:
roughly speaking,
because inline-assembly events are left intact,
the correctness of merging follows essentially
the same arguments as those presented by
Lahav et al. (2017)
.
